Dear CCE Team,
This response serves to formally and finally reiterate the following:

I have already submitted a comprehensive, well-documented, and legally compliant record of the misrepresentations, contract breaches, and deceptive business practices that occurred. This includes detailed timelines, evidence-backed complaints, and agency filings - all more than sufficient to support regulatory protection and legal remedy. Further requests for explanation are not only unnecessary but may be construed as evasive delay.
I have already provided more than sufficient legal and factual basis - through detailed documentation, timelines, and compliance-based filings - that clearly demonstrate your misrepresentations, breach of contract, and unethical business conduct. No further explanation is legally required, nor ethically warranted.
Additionally, the fact that payment was taken before the agreement was sent or signed constitutes a clear procedural and legal violation. Under standard contract law principles and FTC regulations, for any agreement to be enforceable, there must be mutual assent, full disclosure, and consideration. Charging a customer prior to furnishing the contract - especially under high-pressure sales conditions - renders the agreement voidable due to lack of informed consent and constitutes an unfair and deceptive act under federal and state law. (documented and timestamped)
FTC Act – Section 5 (15 U.S. Code § 45)
Prohibits: “Unfair or deceptive acts or practices in or affecting commerce.”
Application: Taking payment before disclosing or presenting a binding agreement qualifies as deceptive inducement. The consumer cannot provide informed consent if material terms are not disclosed prior to payment.
 Reference:
“A practice is deceptive if it misleads or is likely to mislead the consumer, and affects their decision or conduct regarding a product or service.”
		- FTC Policy Statement on Deception

2. Contract Law – Mutual Assent Doctrine
A valid contract requires:
Offer
Acceptance
Consideration
Mutual assent (meeting of the minds)
Violation: Taking payment prior to showing terms eliminates the opportunity for informed assent, rendering the contract voidable or unenforceable under general U.S. contract law.
Restatement (Second) of Contracts §17:
“The formation of a contract requires a bargain in which there is a manifestation of mutual assent to the exchange.”

3. Electronic Signatures in Global and National Commerce Act (E-SIGN Act)
Requirement: If an agreement is formed electronically, the consumer must be:
Clearly presented with the contract
Given the opportunity to review before paying
Violation: Prepayment without first being shown terms is a breach of this requirement and may void consent under the act.
15 U.S. Code § 7001(c)(1)(A):
“The consumer must affirmatively consent to the use of an electronic record... after receiving a clear and conspicuous statement of the terms.”

4. Truth in Lending Act (TILA) – 15 U.S. Code § 1601 et seq.
In cases where credit or installment payments are involved, TILA requires full disclosure of terms prior to any payment obligation.
Violation: Accepting money before the consumer sees financing or refund terms can constitute a violation of fair disclosure requirements.

Furthermore, collecting payment before furnishing or obtaining agreement to the actual contract constitutes a deceptive and unfair practice under 15 U.S. Code § 45 (FTC Act) and violates core tenets of contract law, including the requirement of mutual assent. It may also constitute noncompliance with the E-SIGN Act (15 U.S. Code § 7001) and, if applicable, the Truth in Lending Act (15 U.S. Code § 1601). No party can consent to an agreement they have not been given the opportunity to review. As such, the contract is voidable and any “no-refund” clauses are legally unenforceable.


To be clear:
I received $0 in funding directly or indirectly as a result of your program, coaching, or materials.
The limited funding I obtained occurred independently and outside your system, after your processes demonstrably failed consistently.
Your repeated attempts to claim credit for these results - despite clear, documented evidence to the contrary - are knowingly false, deceptive, and in bad faith.

Your documented refusal to address the misrepresentations and performance failures already noted reinforces the regulatory evidence of deceptive inducement and nondelivery.
Your response demonstrates a pattern of shifting blame, reframing failures as participant fault, and refusing to take accountability for misleading advertising, high-pressure sales tactics, and the failure to deliver what was contractually promised. This aligns with the legal standard for intentional misrepresentation and unfair/deceptive business practices, which have already been reported to federal agencies for investigation.
Your invocation of a no-refund clause, despite clear material breaches and deceptive inducement, is not enforceable when the foundational premise of the agreement was undermined by your own conduct. Further, the assertion that you’ve “suffered financial loss” is wholly misplaced - this was not an earned fee, but a payment obtained under false pretenses, with no delivery of promised results or value.
Under FTC and state law, contracts obtained through fraud or material misrepresentation are voidable and unenforceable, regardless of refund clauses. The presence of deceptive inducement nullifies any claim of mutual consent or consideration.
At this point, any further denial or deflection only strengthens the legal case and increases exposure. The most ethical, lawful, and reputationally sound path forward is immediate full refund.


In sum, this is not a dispute over preferences - it is a documented case of program failure, deceptive marketing, coerced retention, and illegal retaliation for a protected review. Your continued denial only escalates liability.

Direct Responses:
“You obtained $80K in funding”
This claim is factually false and legally reckless. There is no verifiable or traceable record linking that figure to your program or support. It appears designed to distract from your delivery failures. This was already clarified repeatedly and ignored.
I did not receive $80K in funding through your system, and this claim is an attempt to retroactively assign results you had no part in producing. I already made this clear repeatedly. Every attempt using your guidance and “data points” has resulted in failures consistently. I had to intentionally go against everything you guys taught, and went back to what I was doing before you guys and got results. Your stuff is not up to date and sharp enough with the current climate. Stated income levels are way too high, causing income verification and identity verification. The fact that you guys don’t know the actual state income level caps as each institution is also telling. Your denial of stated income levels being higher is just further confirmation that you guys are out of the loop with what is happening at the banks. Clearly explains why you guys don’t operate on a performance fee structure. Also your whole flex of a list of 9100+ banks leading people to believe you have all these relationships to join is ridiculous, especially considering that you have less than 10 RMs. That also don’t have the ability to override income verification. Misleading again.
"The agreement says all sales are final"
The agreement cannot override federal consumer protections. A no-refund clause is not enforceable in cases of fraudulent inducement, non-delivery, retaliation, or misrepresentation. All of these are clearly documented.

You were given multiple Zoom calls and missed group calls
I also had a Zoom call scheduled that Brandon missed. The group calls were repetitive, unhelpful, and unrelated to the core offer promised. I stopped attending because they provided no functional value.

We will take the financial loss and cancel your remaining payments"
This statement is not only false - it’s manipulative. You received $20,600 without providing the promised service. That is not a financial loss; that is unearned revenue obtained through misrepresentation. The suggestion that ceasing further harm is a "loss" reveals your underlying exploitative intent. Further, there is no new infrastructure, team cost, or time spent attributable solely to me - your model depends on funneling customers into a templated system already running. Claiming loss for services never rendered is invalid.
This statement is factually and ethically absurd. You collected $20,600 from me for non-performing, misrepresented services. There is no financial loss on your end; the only one harmed was me. You cannot fudge direct damages solely from me; as nothing new has been implemented because of this. You plugged me into an already existing system. When you have 300+ members, feeding you cash + cash flow.
You’ve attempted to justify the collection of $20,600 on the basis that it "fed your machine" — coaching time, platform fees, and team support. That logic is circular and corrupt. You cannot claim someone’s money funds your operations if no reciprocal value was provided.
You are not entitled to retain funds when your offer failed, your representations were misleading, and your response to negative but truthful feedback was to strip access and silence the customer.
I reiterate: I was misled. Despite your denials, my experience is legally and ethically valid. Every step in this interaction has been documented and submitted to federal regulators.

"You were removed due to causing discomfort to others"
I shared truthful experiences and expressed factual complaints. If others were uncomfortable, it was due to their reflection in the mirror of those facts. 
The retaliation for my review is a direct violation of:
The Consumer Review Fairness Act of 2016, which protects consumers from contractual clauses that restrict honest reviews.
The FTC Act Section 5, which prohibits unfair or deceptive acts, including suppression of truthful public feedback.
These protections exist to prevent exactly the type of silencing and retaliation your team engaged in.

You made assumptions about Brandon"
I did not make baseless assumptions; I shared truthful observations about how the business is run, and how misrepresentation and manipulation are baked into the culture and communication.
You guys are literally teaching and guiding people to lie and commit fraud, and misrepresent information, intention, purpose, and identity to banks, people, and bureaus.  Your mental gymnastics and mental manipulation can’t dance around the truth. This is in your fundamental nature, and you are teaching it.



This has been documented with the CFPB, FTC, and relevant state agencies as part of an ongoing pattern of deceptive practices.
This matter has already drawn the attention of multiple regulatory bodies. Should resolution not be reached promptly, it may proceed to formal legal action or regulatory enforcement. That path benefits no one. The ethical and intelligent decision is to issue a full refund now to avoid further exposure and accountability.
This communication and all supporting evidence are being reviewed by consumer attorneys to ensure full compliance and regulatory enforcement if necessary.
This is a final opportunity to resolve this cooperatively. I respectfully urge you to issue the full refund and close this matter in good faith, rather than continue compounding liabilities that will inevitably be reviewed externally.
If this cannot be resolved within 3 additional business days, I may need to consider other appropriate next steps to ensure the matter is addressed through the proper channels. My hope is to avoid that by working together in good faith.

Respectfully,
Alan Nguyen

Given the opportunity to review before paying
Violation: Prepayment without first being shown terms is a breach of this requirement and may void consent under the act.
15 U.S. Code § 7001(c)(1)(A):
“The consumer must affirmatively consent to the use of an electronic record... after receiving a clear and conspicuous statement of the terms.”

4. Truth in Lending Act (TILA) – 15 U.S. Code § 1601 et seq.
In cases where credit or installment payments are involved, TILA requires full disclosure of terms prior to any payment obligation.
Violation: Accepting money before the consumer sees financing or refund terms can constitute a violation of fair disclosure requirements.

Furthermore, collecting payment before furnishing or obtaining agreement to the actual contract constitutes a deceptive and unfair practice under 15 U.S. Code § 45 (FTC Act) and violates core tenets of contract law, including the requirement of mutual assent. It may also constitute noncompliance with the E-SIGN Act (15 U.S. Code § 7001) and, if applicable, the Truth in Lending Act (15 U.S. Code § 1601). No party can consent to an agreement they have not been given the opportunity to review. As such, the contract is voidable and any “no-refund” clauses are legally unenforceable.

You cannot fudge direct damages solely from me; as nothing new has been implemented because of this. You plugged me into an already existing system. When you have 300+ members, feeding you cash + cash flow.
This cancellation is an indirect admission that continued value does not exist. It further confirms non-performance and breach of promise.
You’ve attempted to justify the collection of $20,600 on the basis that it "fed your machine" - coaching time, platform fees, and team support. That logic is circular and corrupt. You cannot claim someone’s money funds your operations if no reciprocal value was provided.

“You made assumptions about Brandon"
I did not make baseless assumptions; I shared truthful observations about how the business is run, and how misrepresentation and manipulation are baked into the culture and communication.

This conduct violates multiple federal protections:

FTC Act – Section 5 (15 U.S. Code § 45):
Prohibits unfair or deceptive acts, including the suppression of truthful information and retaliatory behavior that chills consumer speech.
Unfair or Deceptive Acts or Practices (UDAP):
State-level laws adopted from the FTC Act that prohibit any pattern of conduct that misleads, harms, or silences consumers.
By attempting to fabricate a public perception of satisfaction, silencing lawful feedback, and retaliating when exposed, your actions demonstrate a willful pattern of deceptive, retaliatory, and unethical behavior. This pattern is now documented, reported, and under regulatory review.

## 1. **Understand Visa’s Dispute Process**

Key rules relevant to your case:  
- **Visa Rule 11.3.4** → A merchant cannot require you to waive chargeback rights in a contract.  
- **Visa Rule 12.5.3.2** → Chargebacks are valid if the merchant misrepresented, failed to disclose terms, or delivered services not as described.  
- **Visa Rule 5.4.2.5** → Refund/cancellation policies must be disclosed *before payment* to be enforceable.

   - **Violation:**  
     - Visa Core Rule **5.4.2.5** – Refund policies must be disclosed before payment.  
     - **Mutual Assent Doctrine (Restatement §17)** – Contracts require assent before payment.  
     - **E-SIGN Act § 7001(c)** – Consent must follow pre-disclosure of terms.

   - **Violation:**

## 🔑 Core Legal Position
1. **Payment Before Terms = Contract Void**
   - **Timestamped Proof**: Payment at 4:42 PM; contract delivered 4:46 PM.

     - **Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5** – Refund policies must be disclosed **before payment.**  
     - **Restatement (Second) of Contracts §17** – No mutual assent if terms come after consideration.  
     - **E-SIGN Act §7001(c)(1)(A)** – Consent must follow clear disclosure.  
     - **FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45)** – Charging before disclosure = unfair/deceptive act.  
   - **Impact**: Any “contract” or “terms” Navy/merchant relies on is void. They cannot enforce “access to portal” or “no-refund clause.”  

2. **Access ≠ Delivery**
   - Visa’s **12.5.3.2 Misrepresentation/Non-Disclosure** recognizes “nondelivery” and “false claims” even if the merchant provides *something.*  
   - **Legal Principle**: Token/partial access does not cure fraud if the **core promised deliverable was never provided.** (UCC §2-302 unconscionability).  
   - You were promised funding results ($300K+), not just videos or PDFs. Delivering “portal access” is not delivering the advertised service.  

3. **Misrepresentation**
   - Ads promised **“$300K+ in 30–90 days”** (even without the 90 explicitly in one ad, oral promises + context matter).

4. **Coercion + Retaliation**

---

## 1. Why Navy Is Defaulting to the Merchant’s Position
- **Banks default to the “in writing” agreement**: Disputes teams often give heavy weight to a signed contract/terms, even if you have proof of deception, because it’s the cleanest evidence for them to defend themselves with if Visa audits them.  
- **They are not incentivized to fight**: A chargeback means Navy eats liability until Visa settles it. It’s cheaper and easier for them to side with the merchant unless they *must* escalate.  
- **Internal attorneys are risk-averse**: Their “attorney review” almost certainly looked at the written contract and ignored verbal misreps, ads, and coercion. They’ll claim, “customer had access, merchant delivered *something*,” and push denial unless forced by law.  

---

## 2. Why They’re Saying “Delivered”
- **Access = delivery in their eyes**: Even if useless, just giving you a login or course is treated as “service rendered.”  
- **They ignore performance/fraud**: They avoid weighing *quality* or *success rates* because that opens liability.  
- **They downplay retaliation**: Revoked access after your review = textbook retaliation, but Navy isn’t recognizing this because it requires effort and escalation beyond their default playbook.  

---

## 3. Why They Recharged Your Card
- When a provisional credit is reversed (because the merchant “won”), they post the debit back. It spikes your utilization — and yes, it’s brutal.  
- This isn’t “criminal” in the criminal-law sense, but it can be **unfair, negligent, and reckless** handling of your FCBA rights.  
- By law, FCBA requires banks to investigate **in good faith**. If they ignore misrepresentation, coercion, and retaliation evidence, they are opening themselves to regulatory liability.  

---

## 4. Why They Mentioned “Court”
- **Strategic deflection**: Suggesting “you’ll need to go to court” is their way of closing the case without admitting noncompliance.  
- **Reality**: You don’t need to jump to court right away. You escalate to Visa Compliance + CFPB (which you already did), and then — only if still denied — you can bring in small claims/federal district court.

- **Misrepresentation & non-delivery** (Visa Core Rule 12.5.3.2).  
- **Coercion & retaliation** (Visa Core Rule 11.3.4, ECOA, FCRA, FTC Act, CRFA).

The previously submitted documentation includes time-stamped proof that payment was processed at 4:42 PM, while the contract containing refund/limitation terms was not presented until 4:46 PM. This is a critical violation. Under:

• Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5 – Refund/cancellation policies must be disclosed before payment.  
• Visa Core Rule 11.3.4 – Merchants cannot waive or suppress chargeback rights.  
• Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 – Misrepresentation/Non-Disclosure is a valid chargeback condition.  
• FCBA §1666 – Billing errors include charges where terms were withheld or misrepresented.  
• Restatement (Second) of Contracts §17 – Contracts require mutual assent before consideration.  
• E-SIGN Act §7001(c)(1)(A) – Consent must follow clear pre-disclosure of terms.  

No agreement signed after payment can override cardholder protections or be used as valid evidence to deny refund. The merchant’s “no-refund” clause is therefore legally void and unenforceable.

This foundational defect undercuts the only defense the merchant can assert (“services delivered under contract”). • Willful noncompliance with FCBA §1666,  
• Reckless disregard of Visa Core Rules 5.4.2.5, 11.3.4, and 12.5.3.2, and  
• Negligent handling of a billing error claim.

I respectfully request escalation to Navy Federal’s legal/compliance team for immediate chargeback approval consistent with Visa and federal consumer protection law. Please confirm escalation and final review by Friday, August 1st, to ensure full FCBA timeline compliance.

Here’s the breakdown for the **Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA) timelines** — and how they apply to you (since today is Tuesday, **August 26, 2025**):  

---

### **Key FCBA Timelines**
1. **Initial Notice Deadline (60 days)**  
   - You must notify your bank of a billing error within **60 days of the statement date** that first showed the error (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)).  
   - You did that — you filed your dispute in May 2025, well within the deadline.

2. **Acknowledgment of Dispute (30 days)**  
   - The creditor (Navy Federal) must acknowledge receipt of your dispute **within 30 days** of receiving it, unless they resolve it in that time.  

3. **Resolution Deadline (2 full billing cycles / max 90 days)**  
   - Navy Federal must investigate and resolve the dispute **within two billing cycles (but not more than 90 days)** after receiving your dispute notice.  
   - “Resolve” means either correcting the error (refund/chargeback) or sending a written explanation why they believe the charge is correct.  

---

### **Applying to Your Case**
- **Transaction Date:** April 25, 2025  
- **Original Dispute:** Filed May 21, 2025 (timely under the 60-day rule).  
- **Today:** August 26, 2025.  

👉 By law, Navy must have **completed their investigation and given you a final determination no later than ~August 21, 2025 (90 days from May 21).**  

This means:  
- If they have not issued a final lawful determination already, they are **out of statutory compliance right now.**  
- Any continued delays, vague responses, or failure to process within this window can itself be reported to the **CFPB as a violation of FCBA timelines**.

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Great question — here’s the nuance:  

---

## 1. **FCBA Timeline vs. Appeal**
- **FCBA §1666** requires the bank to investigate and resolve a billing error **within 90 days** of the dispute notice.  
- If they issued a **written final determination** (even wrongly siding with the merchant), they have technically “met” the 90-day requirement — even if their reasoning is flawed.  
- So if they already told you in writing “we side with the merchant” earlier, they are not *timing-out of compliance*. Instead, your case shifts into **appeal/escalation** or **error in handling**.  

---

## 2. **Appeals and Continuing Rights**
- FCBA doesn’t explicitly provide a *formal “appeal” process*. Once they issue a determination, their statutory duty is technically complete.  
- HOWEVER — if you show that their determination was based on **failure to consider key evidence** (like payment before disclosure, retaliation, misrepresentation), you can still:  
  - Argue **negligent or reckless handling** (failure to investigate in good faith).  
  - Escalate to Visa directly (compliance review).  
  - Escalate to CFPB/FTC (complaint that Navy’s “investigation” was defective).  

---

## 3. **Why They Are Siding with the Merchant**
- They’re using the **“you got access to the course materials”** argument to say services were “delivered.”  
- They’re leaning on the **signed contract/no-refund clause** (even though you signed it post-payment).  
- They ignore oral misrepresentations and deceptive ads because they want written, “objective” proof.  

This is *standard defensive posture* banks use to deny disputes where a merchant waves around a contract.  

---

## 4. **How You Still Win**
You shift the focus away from *subjective dissatisfaction* and hammer **hard, procedural violations** that override contract terms:  
- **Payment-before-terms** = invalid contract (Restatement §17, Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5, E-SIGN §7001(c)).  
- **Merchant coercion to cancel first dispute** = retaliation, voids Navy’s denial reliance (Visa Rule 11.3.4).  
- **Retaliation after review** = violation of CRFA (15 U.S.C. §45b).  
- **Failure to consider these in their investigation** = reckless disregard, negligent investigation (grounds for CFPB enforcement).  

So the key is not arguing *late resolution*, but arguing **wrongful resolution due to willful neglect of evidence and federal protections.**  

---

✅ **Recommendation for Messaging Navy Now (Appeal Mode):**
You want something like:  

> “While Navy issued a prior determination, that decision failed to consider critical, time-stamped evidence that renders the merchant’s contract void (payment before disclosure) and documented retaliation that independently qualifies under FCBA §1666 and Visa Rule 11.3.4. A determination that ignores such evidence constitutes reckless disregard and negligent handling of my billing error claim. I am requesting immediate escalation to Navy’s legal/compliance team and Visa compliance for correction. This matter is also part of CFPB Case #240426-14545424 and FTC Report #190055885.”  

---

⚖️ **Bottom line:**  
- You can’t argue Navy is “late” if they already denied once.  
- You *can* argue their denial was defective, negligent, and noncompliant — which puts them on the hook with CFPB/Visa oversight.

Delivery must align with advertised promises, not mere access to a portal.

Merchant Policy Cannot Override Law
Navy’s conclusion that “the merchant did not violate their policy” is misplaced. Visa Core Rule 5.4.2.5 requires refund terms to be disclosed before payment. My evidence proves payment was processed at 4:42 PM, contract/terms at 4:46 PM. Any “no-refund” policy presented post-payment is void. FCBA §1666 and Restatement (Second) of Contracts §17 make clear: no contract is enforceable without mutual assent before consideration.

No $300K in Writing
Even if the $300K in 90 days was communicated verbally, the merchant’s written ads (attached) still misrepresented material outcomes: “$300–500K funding at 0% interest.” FCBA and FTC Act §5 apply equally to verbal and written inducements. Misrepresentation does not require an exact dollar figure or timeframe - it requires that claims were misleading and likely to induce reliance, which is documented here.

Accordingly, Navy’s reliance on portal access, merchant policies, or the absence of a written “90 days” promise does not negate the statutory and Visa Rule violations already proven. The evidence package already submitted exceeds the evidentiary burden under:
FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B) (billing error: services not accepted or delivered as agreed),
Visa Rule 11.3.4 (no suppression of chargeback rights),
Visa Rule 12.5.3.2 (misrepresentation / nondelivery), and
FTC Act §5 (unfair or deceptive acts).

Any continued denial at this stage would constitute willful noncompliance, reckless disregard, and negligent handling of a billing error claim. This case is already documented under CFPB Case #240426-14545424, 250825-23483188, and FTC Report #190055885. I respectfully request immediate escalation to Navy’s legal/compliance team and Visa Compliance for correction and full chargeback approval.
